, then
G\star^{r}(S_{1}+S_{2})=(G\star^{r}S_{1})\star^{r}S_{2}
.

Proof.

S_{1}+S_{2}
is
r
-incident as for any
k\in[0,r)
and any
K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S_{1}+S_{2})
of size
k+2
,
(S_{1}+S_{2})\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K}=S_{1}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K}+S_{2}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K}
is a multiple of
2^{r-k-\delta(k)}
.
S_{2}
is
r
-incident in
G\star^{r}S_{1}
as for any set
K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S_{2})
of size
k+2
,
S_{2}\bullet\Lambda_{G\star^{r}S_{1}}^{K}=S_{2}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K}
. Besides, for any vertices
u,v
,
(S_{1}+S_{2})\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{u,v}=S_{1}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K}+S_{2}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K}
and

\displaystyle u\sim_{(G\star^{r}S_{1})\star^{r}S_{2}}v\penalty\

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \left(u\sim_{G\star^{r}S_{1}}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ S_{2}\bullet\Lambda_{G\star^{r}S_{1}}^{u,v}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}\right)\penalty\

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \left(u\sim_{G}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ S_{1}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{u,v}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ S_{2}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{u,v}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}\right)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \left(u\sim_{G}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ S_{1}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{u,v}+S_{2}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{u,v}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}\right)\qed

Finally, it is easy to see that the multiplicity in
S
can be upperbounded by
2^{r}
: if
G\star^{r}S
is valid, then
G\star^{r}S=G\star^{r}S^{\prime}
, where, for any vertex
u
,
S^{\prime}(u)=S(u)\bmod 2^{r}
.

4.5
Graphical tools

4.5.1
Types with respect to an MLS cover

Definition 18
.

Given a graph
G
, a vertex
u
is of type P
\in
{X, Y, Z,
\bot
} with respect to a MLS cover
\mathcal{M}
, where P is

•

X if for any generator
D
of a minimal local set of
\mathcal{M}
containing
u
,
u\in D\setminus Odd(D)
;

•

Y if for any generator
D
of a minimal local set of
\mathcal{M}
containing
u
,
u\in D\cap Odd(D)
;

•

Z if for any generator
D
of a minimal local of
\mathcal{M}
set containing
u
,
u\in Odd(D)\setminus D
;

•

\bot
otherwise.

For notational simplicity, "with respect to the MLS cover
\mathcal{M}
" may be left out when the MLS cover is clear from the context. We then simply say that a vertex is e.g. of type X. The names X, Y and Z are chosen to match the corresponding Pauli operator in the stabilizers of the graph state. Indeed, a minimal local set
L
is the support of a stabilizer
(-1)^{|G[D]|}X_{D}Z_{Odd(D)}
if it is of dimension 1, and the support of 3 stabilizers
(-1)^{|G[D]|}X_{D}Z_{Odd(D)}
,
(-1)^{|G[D^{\prime}]|}X_{D^{\prime}}Z_{Odd(D^{\prime})}
and
(-1)^{|G[D\Delta D^{\prime}]|}X_{D\Delta D^{\prime}}Z_{Odd(D\Delta D^{\prime})}
if it is of dimension 2 (see
Proposition

29
and
Proposition

34
). It is direct to see that a vertex
u
is attached to a Pauli X (resp. Y, Z) if
u\in D\setminus Odd(D)
(resp.
D\cap Odd(D)
,
Odd(D)\setminus D
). In the case where
L
is of dimension 2, any vertex
u
is attached to the 3 Paulis X, Y and Z (this is proved in
[
65
]
, and is also direct from the proof of
Proposition

34
).

Proposition 48
.

Given a graph
G
and a MLS cover,
a vertex
u
is of type

•

X if it is attached only to Paulis X in the corresponding stabilizers;

•

Y if it is attached only to Paulis Y in the corresponding stabilizers;

•

Z if it is attached only to Paulis Z in the corresponding stabilizers;

•

\bot
otherwise.

More precisely, a vertex
u
is of type
\bot
if it appears in a minimal local set of dimension 2 in
\mathcal{M}
, or appears in at least two minimal local sets of dimension 1, but attached to different Paulis.

The set of vertices of type
\bot
is invariant under LU-equivalence. To show that, we first prove a technical lemma.

Lemma 5
.

Given two graphs
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
and a local unitary
U
such that
|{G_{2}}\rangle=U|{G_{1}}\rangle
, if
L
is a minimal local set of dimension 1 with generators respectively
D_{1}
in
G_{1}
and
D_{2}
in
G_{2}
, then

X_{D_{2}}Z_{Odd_{G_{2}}(D_{2})}=(-1)^{b}UX_{D_{1}}Z_{Odd_{G_{1}}(D_{1})}U^{\dagger}

with some
b\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

According to
Proposition

13
,

{Tr}_{V\setminus L}\left(|{G_{2}}\rangle\left\langle G_{2}\right|\right)=I+(-1)^{|G_{2}[D_{2}]|}X_{D_{2}}Z_{Odd_{G_{2}}(D_{2})}
and

{Tr}_{V\setminus L}\left(U|{G_{1}}\rangle\left\langle G_{1}\right|U^{\dagger}\right)=U\left(I+(-1)^{|G_{1}[D_{1}]|}X_{D_{1}}Z_{Odd_{G_{1}}(D_{1})}\right)U^{\dagger}
.
∎

Lemma 6
.

Two LU-equivalent graphs have the same vertices of type
\bot
(with respect to any MLS cover).

Proof.

Let
U
be a local unitary such that
|{G_{2}}\rangle=U|{G_{1}}\rangle
, and let
u\in V
a vertex of type
\bot
in
G_{1}
. If
u
is in a minimal local set of dimension 2 in
G_{1}
, so is in
G_{2}
as the dimension of minimal local sets is invariant under LU-equivalence. Indeed, two LU-equivalent graphs have the same cut-rank function (see
Proposition

28
) and the dimension of a minimal local set depends solely on the cut-rank function (see
Proposition

34
).

Otherwise, in
G_{1}
,
u
appears in two distinct minimal local sets
L
,
L^{\prime}
of dimension 1,
such that
u
is attached to different Paulis
P
and
P^{\prime}
respectively in
L
and
L^{\prime}
.
According to
Lemma

5
, in
G_{2}
,
v
is attached to Paulis
U_{u}PU_{u}^{\dagger}
and
U_{u}P^{\prime}U_{u}^{\dagger}
in respectively
L
and
L^{\prime}
. Moreover, two Paulis are different when they anticommute, so
U_{u}PU_{u}^{\dagger}
and
U_{u}P^{\prime}U_{u}^{\dagger}
are different Paulis if and only if
P
and
P^{\prime}
are different Paulis.
∎

In
[
65
]
it is shown that for graphs with only vertices of type
\bot
, LU-equivalence implies LC-equivalence, i.e. LU=LC (see
Section

6.2
). Types X, Y and Z are an attempt of generalizing the techniques used in
[
65
]
to all graphs.

4.5.2
Constraints given by the types

We first show that local unitaries mapping one graph state to another are Clifford for vertices contained in a minimal local set of dimension 2. Recall that according to
Proposition

34
, a minimal local set of dimension 2 contains an even number of vertices.

Proposition 49
.

Given two graphs
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
and a local unitary
U
such that
|{G_{2}}\rangle=U|{G_{1}}\rangle
, if
L
is a minimal local set of dimension 2 containing at least 4 vertices, then for any vertex
u\in L
,
U_{u}
is a Clifford operator.

This was first proven in
[
75
]
(see also
[
42
]
) but the original proof involves the qutrit formalism. An alternative proof, that does not use the qutrit formalism, is given in
[
76
]
. In the following, we prove
Proposition

49
similarly than in
[
76
]
. First we prove two technical lemmata.

Lemma 7
.

If
L
is a minimal local set of dimension 2 of a graph
G=(V,E)
, then there exists a local Clifford operator
C
such that
2^{|L|}{Tr}_{V\setminus L}\left(|{G}\rangle\left\langle G\right|\right)=C\left(I_{L}+X_{L}+(-1)^{|L|/2}Y_{L}+Z_{L}\right)C^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

According to
Proposition

13
(see also
Proposition

29
and
Proposition

34
), there exist
D,D^{\prime}\!\subseteq L
such that
2^{|L|}{Tr}_{V\setminus L}\!\left(|{G}\rangle\!\left\langle G\right|\right)\allowbreak\!=\!(-1)^{|G[D]|}X_{D}Z_{Odd(D)}\allowbreak+(-1)^{|G[D\Delta D^{\prime}]|}X_{D\Delta D^{\prime}}Z_{Odd(D\Delta D^{\prime})}\allowbreak+(-1)^{|G[D^{\prime}]|}X_{D^{\prime}}Z_{Odd(D^{\prime})}
. The conjugation of Pauli gates by single-qubit Clifford operators acts as a permutation of the Pauli gates (see Figure
2.2
), up to a sign change. Also, every vertex of
L
is attached to the 3 Paulis X, Y, Z. Thus, there exists a local Clifford operator
C
such that
2^{|L|}{Tr}_{V\setminus L}\left(|{G}\rangle\left\langle G\right|\right)=C\left(I_{L}+X_{L}+(-1)^{b}Y_{L}+Z_{L}\right)C^{\dagger}
where
b\in\{0,1\}
. Finally,
(-1)^{|L|/2}Y_{L}\allowbreak=X_{L}Z_{L}\allowbreak=C^{\dagger}(-1)^{|G[D]|}X_{D}Z_{Odd(D)}(-1)^{|G[D^{\prime}]|}X_{D^{\prime}}Z_{Odd(D^{\prime})}C\allowbreak=C^{\dagger}(-1)^{|G[D\Delta D^{\prime}]|}X_{D\Delta D^{\prime}}Z_{Odd(D\Delta D^{\prime})}C\allowbreak=(-1)^{b}Y_{L}
. Thus,
(-1)^{b}=(-1)^{|L|/2}
.
∎

Lemma 8
.

If
U
is a single-qubit unitary and
P\in\{X,Y,Z\}
, then
UPU^{\dagger}=c_{PX}X+c_{PY}Y+c_{PZ}Z
, where the
c_{PP^{\prime}}
are complex numbers satisfying
|c_{PX}|^{2}+|c_{PY}|^{2}+|c_{PZ}|^{2}=1
. Additionally,
U
is Clifford if and only if, for any
P\in\{X,Y,Z\}
, there is
P^{\prime}\in\{X,Y,Z\}
such that
|c_{PP^{\prime}}|=1
(implying
c_{PP^{\prime\prime}}=0
for
P^{\prime\prime}\in\{X,Y,Z\}\setminus\{P^{\prime}\}
).

Proof.

\{I,X,Y,Z\}
form a basis of the
2\times 2
matrices, thus
UPU^{\dagger}=c_{PI}I+c_{PX}X+c_{PY}Y+c_{PZ}Z
. As the trace is linear and
Tr(UPU^{\dagger})=Tr(X)=Tr(Y)=Tr(Z)=0
, then
c_{PI}=0
. Also,
Tr((UPU^{\dagger})(UPU^{\dagger})^{\dagger})=Tr(P^{2})=Tr(I)=2
and
Tr((c_{PX}X+c_{PY}Y+c_{PZ}Z)(c_{PX}X+c_{PY}Y+c_{PZ}Z)^{\dagger})=|c_{PX}|^{2}Tr(I)+|c_{PY}|^{2}Tr(I)+|c_{PZ}|^{2}Tr(I)=2(|c_{PX}|^{2}+|c_{PY}|^{2}+|c_{PZ}|^{2})
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

49
.

According to
Proposition

34
,
L
contains an even number of vertices. Thus, we write
L=2m
(where
m\geqslant 2
). Thus,
{Tr}_{V\setminus L}\left(U|{G_{1}}\rangle\left\langle G_{1}\right|U^{\dagger}\right)={Tr}_{V\setminus L}\left(|{G_{2}}\rangle\left\langle G_{2}\right|\right)
. According to
Lemma

7
,

\displaystyle UC_{1}\left(I_{L}+X_{L}+(-1)^{m}Y_{L}+Z_{L}\right)C_{1}^{\dagger}U^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=C_{2}\left(I_{L}+X_{L}+(-1)^{m}Y_{L}+Z_{L}\right)C_{2}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle U^{\prime}\left(I_{L}+X_{L}+(-1)^{m}Y_{L}+Z_{L}\right)U^{\prime\dagger}

\displaystyle=I_{L}+X_{L}+(-1)^{m}Y_{L}+Z_{L}

\displaystyle U^{\prime}\left(X_{L}+(-1)^{m}Y_{L}+Z_{L}\right)U^{\prime\dagger}

\displaystyle=X_{L}+(-1)^{m}Y_{L}+Z_{L}

where
C_{1},C_{2}
are local Clifford operators and
U^{\prime}=C_{2}^{\dagger}UC_{1}
. Note that
U^{\prime}
is Clifford if and only if
U
is Clifford. With the notation
U^{\prime}=e^{i\phi}\bigotimes_{u\in V}U^{\prime}_{u}
, we can write, according to
Lemma

8
, for
P,P^{\prime}\in\{X,Y,Z\}
,
U^{\prime}_{u}P{U^{\prime}}_{u}^{\dagger}=c^{(u)}_{PX}X+c^{(u)}_{PY}Y+c^{(u)}_{PZ}Z
where
|c^{(u)}_{PX}|^{2}+|c^{(u)}_{PY}|^{2}+|c^{(u)}_{PZ}|^{2}=1
.

\displaystyle U^{\prime}\left(X_{L}+(-1)^{m}Y_{L}+Z_{L}\right)U^{\prime\dagger}

\displaystyle=U^{\prime}X_{L}U^{\prime\dagger}+(-1)^{m}U^{\prime}Y_{L}U^{\prime\dagger}+U^{\prime}Z_{L}U^{\prime\dagger}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\textbf{P}}\in\{X,Y,Z\}^{L}}\left(\prod_{u\in L}c^{(u)}_{X{\textbf{P}_{u}}}+(-1)^{m}\prod_{u\in L}c^{(u)}_{Y{\textbf{P}_{u}}}+\prod_{u\in L}c^{(u)}_{Z{\textbf{P}_{u}}}\right){\textbf{P}}

\displaystyle=X_{L}+(-1)^{m}Y_{L}+Z_{L}

As
\{I,X,Y,Z\}
form a basis of the
2\times 2
matrices,

\sum_{P^{\prime}\in\{X,Y,Z\}}\left|\prod_{u\in L}c^{(u)}_{XP^{\prime}}+(-1)^{m}\prod_{u\in L}c^{(u)}_{YP^{\prime}}+\prod_{u\in L}c^{(u)}_{ZP^{\prime}}\right|=3

The triangle inequality implies

\sum_{P,P^{\prime}\in\{X,Y,Z\}}\prod_{u\in L}|c^{(u)}_{PP^{\prime}}|\geqslant 3

Conversely, the Hölder’s inequality (see for example
[
77
]
) implies

\displaystyle\sum_{P,P^{\prime}\in\{X,Y,Z\}}\prod_{u\in L}|c^{(u)}_{PP^{\prime}}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in\{X,Y,Z\}}\left(\prod_{u\in L}|c^{(u)}_{PX}|+\prod_{u\in L}|c^{(u)}_{PY}|+\prod_{u\in L}|c^{(u)}_{PZ}|\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{P\in\{X,Y,Z\}}\left(\prod_{u\in L}\left(|c^{(u)}_{PX}|^{2m}+|c^{(u)}_{PY}|^{2m}+|c^{(u)}_{PZ}|^{2m}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2m}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{P\in\{X,Y,Z\}}\left(\prod_{u\in L}\left(|c^{(u)}_{PX}|^{2}+|c^{(u)}_{PY}|^{2}+|c^{(u)}_{PZ}|^{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2m}}=3

As
m\geqslant 2
, this is an equality if and only
|c^{(u,v)}_{PP^{\prime}}|=
0 or 1 for every
u,v,P,P^{\prime}
. Thus, according to
Lemma

8
, for any vertex
u\in L
,
U_{u}
is a Clifford operator.
∎

Both minimal local sets of dimension 1 and 2 give constraints on local unitaries mapping one graph state to the other.

Proposition 50
.

If
G_{1}=_{LU}G_{2}
, then there exists
U=e^{i\phi}\bigotimes_{u\in V}U_{u}
such that
|{G_{2}}\rangle=U|{G_{1}}\rangle
and:

•

U_{u}
is a Clifford operator if
u
is of type
\bot
in both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
;

•

U_{u}=X(\theta_{u})Z^{b_{u}}
if
u
is of type X in both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
;

•

U_{u}=Z(\theta_{u})X^{b_{u}}
if
u
is of type Z in both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
.

with
b_{u}\in\{0,1\}
. Additionally, if

G_{1}=_{LC_{r}}G_{2}
,
U
can be chosen such that every angle satisfies
\theta_{u}=0\bmod\pi/2^{r}
.

Proof.

If
u
is of type X, let
L
be a minimal local set such that
u\in L
. According to
Lemma

5
,
U_{u}XU_{u}^{\dagger}=e^{i\phi}X
.
U_{u}|{+}\rangle
and
U_{u}|{-}\rangle
are eigenvectors of
U_{u}XU_{u}^{\dagger}
with eigenvalues respectively 1 and -1, implying
U_{u}XU_{u}^{\dagger}=\pm X
. If
U_{u}XU_{u}^{\dagger}=X
, then
U_{u}=e^{i\phi}X(\theta)
. If
U_{u}XU_{u}^{\dagger}=-X
, define
{U^{\prime}}\vcentcolon=U_{u}Z
.
{U^{\prime}}X{U^{\prime}}^{\dagger}=X
, so
{U^{\prime}}=e^{i\phi}X(\theta)
, thus
U_{u}=e^{i\phi}X(\theta)Z
. The proof is similar when
u
is of type Z.

If
u
is of type
\bot
,
u
appears in a minimal local set of dimension 2, or it appears in two minimal local sets of dimension 1, but with different Paulis. If
u
appears in a minimal local set of dimension 2 containing at least 4 vertices, according to
Proposition

49
,
U_{u}
is Clifford. If
u
appears in a minimal local set of dimension 2 containing exactly 2 vertices
u
and
v
, then
u
is part of a connected component containing exactly 2 adjacent vertices. Indeed, the only possible generators are
\{u\}
,
\{v\}
, and
\{u,v\}
, implying that
u
is the only neighbor of
v
, and vice versa. As the only graph LU-equivalent to
K_{2}
(the complete graph with two vertices) is
K_{2}
itself,
U_{u}
(and
U_{v}
) can be chosen to be the identity without loss of generality.

Suppose now that
u
appears in two minimal local sets
L
and
L^{\prime}
of dimension 1 with different Paulis. Then
{U}P_{1}{U}^{\dagger}=\pm P_{2}
and
{U}P^{\prime}_{1}{U}^{\dagger}=\pm P^{\prime}_{2}
with Paulis
P_{1},P^{\prime}_{1},P_{2},P^{\prime}_{2}
such that
P_{1}\neq P^{\prime}_{1}
and thus
P_{2}\neq P^{\prime}_{2}
. Also,
UP_{1}P^{\prime}_{1}U^{\dagger}=\pm P_{2}P^{\prime}_{2}
. This means that
U_{u}
is Clifford.
∎

4.5.3
Standard form

When a local complementation is applied on a vertex
u
, its type remains unchanged if it is X or
\bot
, while types Y and Z are swapped. For the neighbors of
u
, types Z and
\bot
remain unchanged, whereas X and Y are exchanged. This leads to a notion of standard form:

Definition 19
.

A graph
G
is in standard form with respect to a MLS cover
\mathcal{M}
if:

•

There are no vertices of type Y with respect to
\mathcal{M}
;

•

For every vertex
u
of type X with respect to
\mathcal{M}
, any neighbor
v
of
u
is of type Z with respect to
\mathcal{M}
and satisfies
u<v
, in particular the vertices of type X with respect to
\mathcal{M}
form an independent set;

•

For every vertex
u
of type X with respect to
\mathcal{M}
,
\{u\}\cup N_{G}(u)\in\mathcal{M}
.

An illustration of the standard form is given in Figure
4.4
.

\bm{X}
\bm{Z}
\bm{\bot}

Figure 4.4
:
Illustration of a graph in standard form. Vertices of type X form an independent set, and are only adjacent to vertices of type Z. There are no vertices of type Y.

Remark 5
.

This notion of standard form is a generalization of the one introduced in
[
2
]
, where the MLS cover considered consists of every minimal local set of the graph:
\mathcal{M}_{\text{max}}\vcentcolon=\{L\subseteq V\penalty\ |\penalty\ L\text{\penalty\ is a minimal local set}\}
. Since there can be exponentially many minimal local sets, using
\mathcal{M}_{\text{max}}
does not lead to an efficient procedure, for instance when computing the type of each vertex.

Given a pair of LU-equivalent graphs, one can efficiently compute a (common) MLS cover and put both graphs in standard form with respect to this MLS cover, by means of local complementations:

Lemma 9
.

There exists an efficient algorithm that takes as inputs two graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, and either claim that they are not LU-equivalent, or compute an MLS cover
\mathcal{M}
and two graphs
G^{\prime}_{1}=_{LC}G_{1}
and
G^{\prime}_{2}=_{LC}G_{2}
, such that
G^{\prime}_{1}
and
G^{\prime}_{2}
are both in standard form with respect to
\mathcal{M}
, in runtime
O(n^{6.38})
.

Proof.

To prove the proposition, we introduce an algorithm that transforms the input graphs
G_{1},G_{2}
into graphs in standard form with respect to the same MLS cover by means of local complementations. The notation
\wedge
refers to the pivoting operation:
G\wedge uv\vcentcolon=G\star u\star v=G\star v\star u
.
The action of the local complementation and the pivoting on the type of the vertices is given in the following table (the types of the unwritten vertices remain unchanged).

Type of
u
in

\penalty\ \penalty\ G\penalty\ \penalty\

G\star u

X

X

Y

Z

Z

Y

\bot

\bot

Type of
v{\in}N_{G}(u)
in

\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ G\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

G\star u

X

Y

Y

X

Z

Z

\bot

\bot

Type of
u
(or
v
) in

\penalty\ \penalty\ G\penalty\ \penalty\

G\wedge uv

X

Z

Y

Y

Z

X

\bot

\bot

The algorithm reads as follows:

1.

Compute an MLS cover
\mathcal{M}
of
G_{1}
(see
Theorem

3
). If
\mathcal{M}
is not an MLS cover of
G_{2}
, then
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are not LU-equivalent.

2.

If there is an XX-edge (i.e. an edge
uv
such that both
u
and
v
are of type X with respect to
\mathcal{M}
) in
G_{1}
or
G_{2}
: apply a pivoting on it.

Repeat until there is no XX-edge left.

3.

If there is an XY-edge in
G_{1}
or
G_{2}
: apply a local complementation on the vertex of type X, then go to step 2.

4.

If there is a vertex of type Y in
G_{1}
or
G_{2}
: apply a local complementation on it, then go to step 2.

5.

If there is an X
\bot
-edge in
G_{1}
or
G_{2}
: apply a pivoting on it.

Repeat until there is no X
\bot
-edge left.

6.

If there is an XZ-edge
uv
in
G_{1}
or
G_{2}
such that
v<u
: apply a pivoting on
uv
.

Repeat until for every XZ-edge
uv
in
G_{1}
or
G_{2}
,
u<v
.

7.

If there is a vertex
u
of type X in
G_{1}
(resp.
G_{2}
) such that
\{u\}\cup N_{G_{1}}(u)
(resp.
\{u\}\cup N_{G_{2}}(u)
) is not a minimal local set of dimension 1: find a minimal local set
M
contained in
\{u\}\cup N_{G_{1}}(u)
(resp.
\{u\}\cup N_{G_{2}}(u)
) and check that
M
is a minimal local set of same dimension in both graphs (if not, they are not LU-equivalent). If this is the case, add
M
to
\mathcal{M}
then go to step 5.

8.

For every vertex
u
of type X in
G_{1}
(resp.
G_{2}
), add
\{u\}\cup N_{G_{1}}(u)
(resp.
\{u\}\cup N_{G_{2}}(u)
) to
\mathcal{M}
.

Correctness.

When step 2 is completed, there is no XX-edge. Step 3 transforms the neighbors of type Y into vertices of type Z. No vertex of type Y is created as there is no XX-edge before the local complementation. When step 3 is completed, there is no XX-edge nor any XY-edge. Step 4 transforms the vertex of type Y into a vertex of type Z. No vertex of type Y is created as there is no XY-edge before the local complementation. When step 4 is completed, there is no vertex of type Y nor any XX-edge. In step 5, applying a pivoting on an X
\bot
-edge transforms the vertex of type X into a vertex of type Z. No XX-edge is created, as the vertex of type X has no neighbor of type X before the pivoting. When step 5 is completed, there is no vertex of type Y and each neighbor of a vertex of type X is of type Z. In step 6, applying a pivoting on an XZ-edge permutes the type of the two vertices, and preserves the fact that each neighbor of a vertex of type X is of type Z. In step 7, adding a minimal local set to
\mathcal{M}
may only change the type of some vertices to
\bot
. When step 7 is completed, for every vertex
u
of type X in
G_{1}
(resp.
G_{2}
),
\{u\}\cup N_{G_{1}}(u)
(resp.
\{u\}\cup N_{G_{2}}(u)
) is a minimal local set. Thus, in step 8, adding those minimal local sets leave the types invariant. When step 8 is completed,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are in standard form with respect to
\mathcal{M}
.

Termination.

The quantity
2|V^{G_{1}}_{Y}|+|V^{G_{1}}_{X}|+2|V^{G_{2}}_{Y}|+|V^{G_{2}}_{X}|
, where
|V^{G_{i}}_{X}|
(resp.
|V^{G_{i}}_{Y}|
) denotes the number of vertices of type X (resp. Y) with respect to
\mathcal{M}
in
G_{i}
, strictly decreases at steps 2 to 5, which guarantees to reach step 6. At step 6,
V^{G_{i}}_{X}
is updated as follows: exactly one vertex
u
is removed from the set and is replaced by a vertex
v
such that
v<u
, which guarantees the termination of step 6. Each time a minimal local set is added to
\mathcal{M}
in step 7, at least one vertex not of type
\bot
becomes of type
\bot
. Indeed, without loss of generality, let
M
be a minimal local set in
\{u\}\cup N_{G_{i}}(u)
, assuming
\{u\}\cup N_{G_{i}}(u)
is not a minimal local set of dimension 1 generated by
\{u\}
. If
M
is of dimension 2, every vertex of
M
becomes of type
\bot
when adding
M
to
\mathcal{M}
. Else,
M
is generated by a set containing at least one vertex of type Z, which becomes of type
\bot
when adding
M
to
\mathcal{M}
.

Complexity.

The time-complexity of the algorithm is given by the time-complexity of step 1, as it is asymptotically the most computationally expensive step. The MLS cover can be computed in runtime
O(n^{6.38})
, where
n
is the order of the graph. It should be noted that giving the type of a vertex with respect to some minimal local set can be done in time-complexity
O(n^{3})
. Indeed, in the case of a minimal local set
L
of dimension 1, finding
D
such that
L=D\cup Odd_{G}(D)
reduces to finding the kernel of some matrix with coefficients in
\mathbb{F}_{2}
(see details in
[
1
]
), which can be done using Gaussian elimination.
∎

Standard form with respect to a common MLS cover implies some strong similarities in the structure of graphs:

Lemma 10
.

If two graphs
G_{1}=_{LU}G_{2}
are in standard form (with respect to the same MLS cover
\mathcal{M}
), then every vertex has the same type in
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, and every vertex
u
of type X satisfies
N_{G_{1}}(u)=N_{G_{2}}(u)
.

Proof.

First, by
Lemma

6
,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
have the same vertices of type
\bot
. Let
u
be a vertex of type X in
G_{1}
and suppose by contradiction that
u
is of type Z in
G_{2}
. By definition of the standard form,
L\vcentcolon=\{u\}\cup N_{G_{1}}(u)
is a minimal local set in
\mathcal{M}
and
u
is the smallest element in
L
(in both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
). Moreover,
L=D\cup Odd_{G_{2}}(D)
and
u\in Odd_{G_{2}}(D)\setminus D
by definition of type Z. Thus,
u
is adjacent to at least one vertex
v\in D
in
G_{2}
.
v
is of type X in
G_{2}
as, by definition of the standard form, there is no vertex of type Y, and
N_{G_{1}}(u)
contains no vertex of type
\bot
. This is a contradiction with the definition of the standard form, as
u<v
. By symmetry, any vertex of type X in
G_{2}
is also of type X in
G_{1}
. Overall, each vertex has the same type in
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
. By definition of the standard form, for any vertex
u
of type X,
L\vcentcolon=\{u\}\cup N_{G_{1}}(u)
is a minimal local set in
\mathcal{M}
, thus there exists
D\subseteq L
such that
L=D\cup Odd_{G_{2}}(D)
. As
D
is non-empty and does not contain any vertex of type Z,
D=\{u\}
, implying that
N_{G_{1}}(u)=N_{G_{2}}(u)
.
∎

After performing the algorithm described in
Lemma

9
, one can check in quadratic time
7
7

7

In the order of the graphs, assuming the information of the types of the vertices with respect to the MLS cover is conserved.
whether each vertex has the same type in
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, and whether every vertex of type X has the same neighborhood in both graphs. If either condition is not met, the graphs are not LU-equivalent.

4.6
Capturing LU-equivalence

Following
Lemma

10
, as two LU-equivalent graphs in standard form (with respect to the same MLS cover) have the same types, we use the notation
V_{X}
,
V_{Z}
and
V_{\bot}
for the set of vertices of type X, Z and
\bot
respectively.

Thanks to the standard form, one can accommodate the types of two LU-equivalent graphs, to simplify the local unitaries mapping one to the other:

Lemma 11
.

If
G_{1}=_{LU}G_{2}
are both in standard form (with respect to the same MLS cover),
there exists
G^{\prime}_{1}=_{LC}G_{1}
in standard form such that
|{G_{2}}\rangle=\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})|{G^{\prime}_{1}}\rangle
.

Remark 6
.

If
G_{1}=_{LC_{r}}G_{2}
, we can choose the angles such that
\alpha_{u},\beta_{v}=0\bmod\pi/2^{r}
. Additionally,
G_{1}
and
G^{\prime}_{1}
are related by local complementations only on vertices of type
\bot
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

50
there exists
U
such that
|{G_{2}}\rangle=U|{G_{1}}\rangle
, with
U=e^{i\phi}\bigotimes_{u\in V}U_{u}
where:

•

C_{u}\vcentcolon=U_{u}
is a Clifford operator if
u\in V_{\bot}
;

•

U_{u}=X(\theta_{u})Z^{b_{u}}
if
u\in V_{X}
;

•

U_{u}=Z(\theta_{u})X^{b_{u}}
if
u\in V_{Z}
.

with
b_{u}\in\{0,1\}
. Additionally,
if
|{G_{2}}\rangle=U|{G_{1}}\rangle
, we choose
U
so that the angles satisfy
\theta_{u}=0\bmod\pi/2^{r}
.

|{G_{2}}\rangle=e^{i\phi}\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})Z^{b_{u}}\bigotimes_{u\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{u})X^{b_{u}}\bigotimes_{u\in\bot}C_{u}|{G_{1}}\rangle

As for any vertex
u\in V
,
X_{u}Z_{N_{G_{1}}(u)}|{G_{1}}\rangle=|{G_{1}}\rangle
, then for possibly different angles
\beta_{u}
(albeit equal modulo
\pi/2
), values
b_{u}
and Clifford operators
C_{u}
,

|{G_{2}}\rangle=e^{i\phi}\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})Z^{b_{u}}\bigotimes_{u\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{u})\bigotimes_{u\in\bot}C_{u}|{G_{1}}\rangle

By applying
\left\langle+\right|_{V_{X}}\left\langle 0\right|_{V_{Z}}
on both sides of the previous equation we get, on the left-hand side,
\left\langle+\right|_{V_{X}}\left\langle 0\right|_{V_{Z}}|{G_{2}}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{|V_{Z}|}}}\left\langle+\right|_{V_{X}}|{+}\rangle_{V_{X}}|{G_{2}[\bot]}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{|V_{Z}|}}}|{G_{2}[\bot]}\rangle
as
V_{X}
is an independent set in
G_{1}
; and, on the right-hand side,
\begin{cases}\frac{e^{i\phi}}{\sqrt{2^{|V_{Z}|}}}\otimes_{u\in\bot}C_{u}|{G_{1}[\bot]}\rangle&\text{if $\forall u\in V_{X},b_{u}=0$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}
, since
\left\langle 0\right|Z(\theta)=\left\langle 0\right|
,
\left\langle+\right|X(\theta)=\left\langle+\right|
and
\left\langle+\right|Z=\left\langle-\right|
. Hence, every
b_{u}=0
, and
|{G_{2}[\bot]}\rangle=e^{i\phi}\bigotimes_{u\in\bot}C_{u}|{G_{1}[\bot]}\rangle
(if
\bot=\emptyset
then
e^{i\phi}=1
).

Using
Proposition

24
, there exist a sequence of (possibly repeating) vertices
a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\in\bot
and a set
D\subseteq\bot
such that
G_{2}[\bot]=G_{1}[\bot]\star a_{1}\star a_{2}\star\cdots\star a_{m}
and
e^{i\phi}\bigotimes_{u\in\bot}C_{u}=L_{a_{m}}\cdots L_{a_{2}}L_{a_{1}}
,
where
L_{a_{i}}\vcentcolon=X\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)_{a_{i}}Z\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)_{N_{{G_{1}}[\bot]\star a_{1}\star\cdots\star a_{i-1}}(a_{i})}
.

These operators do not implement a local complementation on the full graph. For any
i\in[1,m]
, let us introduce

L^{\prime}_{a_{i}}=L_{a_{i}}Z\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)_{N_{G_{1}\star a_{1}\star\cdots\star a_{i-1}}(a_{i})\setminus\bot}=X\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)_{a_{i}}Z\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)_{N_{G_{1}\star a_{1}\star\cdots\star a_{i-1}}(a_{i})}

Then, for possibly different angles
\beta_{u}
(albeit equal modulo
\pi/2
),

\begin{split}|{G_{2}}\rangle&=\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{u\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{u})L^{\prime}_{a_{m}}\cdots L^{\prime}_{a_{2}}L^{\prime}_{a_{1}}|{G_{1}}\rangle\\
&=\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{u\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{u})|{G_{1}\star a_{1}\star\cdots\star a_{m}}\rangle\qed\end{split}

They are strong constraints relating the angles of the X- and Z-rotations acting on different qubits:

Lemma 12
.

Given
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
in standard form (with respect to the same MLS cover),
if
|{G_{2}}\rangle=\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})|{G_{1}}\rangle
:

•

\forall v\in V_{Z},\penalty\ \beta_{v}=-\sum_{u\in N_{G_{1}}(v)\cap V_{X}}\alpha_{u}\bmod 2\pi
;

•

\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\forall K\subseteq V_{Z}
of size
k+2
,
\sum_{u\in\Lambda_{G_{1}}^{K}\cap V_{X}}\alpha_{u}=0\bmod\dfrac{\pi}{2^{k+\delta(k)}}
.

Proof.

According to
Corollary

8
:

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})|{G_{1}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{K\subseteq V,K\neq\emptyset}CZ_{K}\left((-2)^{|K|-1}\sum_{u\in\Lambda_{G_{1}}^{K}\cap V_{X}}\alpha_{u}\right)\right)\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})|{G_{1}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{K\subseteq V,\penalty\ |K|\geqslant 2}CZ_{K}\left((-2)^{|K|-1}\sum_{u\in\Lambda_{G_{1}}^{K}\cap V_{X}}\alpha_{u}\right)\right)\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v}+\sum_{u\in N_{G_{1}}(v)\cap V_{X}}\alpha_{u})|{G_{1}}\rangle

\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})|{G_{1}}\rangle
is a weighted hypergraph state. It is in particular a graph state if and only if the constraints described in
Lemma

12
are met.
∎

The constraints described in
Lemma

12
coincide with
r
-incidence, so, when angles are multiples of
\pi/2^{r}
, this unitary transformation implements an
r
-local complementation.

Lemma 13
.

Given
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
in standard form (with respect to the same MLS cover),

if
|{G_{2}}\rangle=\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})|{G_{1}}\rangle
and
\forall u\in V_{X}
,
\alpha_{u}=0\bmod\pi/2^{r}
, then

\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})
implements an
r
-local complementation over the vertices of
V_{X}
.

Proof.

Let us construct an
r
-incident independent multiset S such that
G_{2}=G_{1}\star^{r}S
. We define
S
on the vertices of
V_{X}
such that
\forall u\in V_{X}
,
\alpha_{u}=\frac{S(u)\pi}{2^{r}}\bmod 2\pi
with
S(u)\in[1,2^{r})
. Note that
\sum_{u\in\Lambda_{G_{1}}^{K}\cap V_{X}}\alpha_{u}=\frac{\pi}{2^{r}}S\bullet\Lambda_{G_{1}}^{K}
. Hence, by
Lemma

12
, For any
k\in[0,r)
, and any
K\subseteq V\setminus S
of size
k+2
,
S\bullet\Lambda_{G_{1}}^{K}
is a multiple of
2^{r-k-\delta(k)}
meaning that S is
r
-incident.
Also, by
Lemma

12
,
\beta_{v}=-\frac{\pi}{2^{r}}\sum_{u\in N_{G_{1}}(v)}S(u)\bmod 2\pi
. Thus,
\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})
implements an
r
-local complementation over
S
, according to
Proposition

44
.
∎

We can now easily relate LC
r
-equivalence to
r
-local complementations for graphs in standard form:

Lemma 14
.

If
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are both in standard form (with respect to the same MLS cover) and
G_{1}=_{LC_{r}}G_{2}
, then
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are related by a sequence of local complementations on the vertices of type
\bot
along with a single
r
-local complementation over the vertices of type X.

Remark 7
.

The sequence of local complementations commutes with the
r
-local complementation, as vertices of type X and vertices of type
\bot
do not share edges by definition of the standard form.

Proof.

By
Lemma

11
, there exists
G^{\prime}_{1}=_{LC}G_{1}
in standard form such that
|{G_{2}}\rangle\\
=\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})|{G^{\prime}_{1}}\rangle
where

\forall u\in V
of type X or Z,
\alpha_{u},\beta_{u}=0\bmod\pi/2^{r}
. By
Lemma

13
,
\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})
implements an
r
-local complementation over the vertices of type X.
∎

Notice in particular that when there is no vertex of type
\bot
, a single
r
-local complementation is required.

Corollary 9
.

If two
\bot
-free LC
r
-equivalent graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are both in standard form (with respect to the same MLS cover), they are related by a single
r
-local complementation over the vertices of type X.

We are ready to prove that
r
-local complementation captures the LC
r
-equivalence of graph states.

Theorem 5
.

Given two graphs
G_{1},G_{2}
, the following statements are equivalent:

1.

|{G_{1}}\rangle
and
|{G_{2}}\rangle
are LC
r
-equivalent.

2.

G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are related by
r
-local complementations.

3.

G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are related by a sequence of local complementations along with a single
r
-local complementation.

Proof.

We proceed by cyclic proof.
(
2\Rightarrow 1
): Follows from
Proposition

44
.
(
3\Rightarrow 2
): A local complementation is, in particular, an
r
-local complementation.
(
1\Rightarrow 3
): Follows from
Lemma

9
along with
Lemma

14
.
∎

From now, we call two graphs LC
r
-equivalent when the corresponding graph states are LC
r
-equivalent or, equivalently, when the graphs are related by a sequence of
r
-local complementations. More than just LC
r
-equivalence,
r
-local complementations can actually characterize the LU-equivalence of graph states.

Theorem 6
.

If two graph states are LU-equivalent, they are LC
r
-equivalent for some
r\leqslant n
, i.e. the corresponding graphs are related by
r
-local complementations.

Proof.

Suppose

G_{1}=_{LU}G_{2}
. By
Lemma

9
along with
Lemma

11
, there exist
G^{\prime}_{1}=_{LC}G_{1}
and
G^{\prime}_{2}=_{LC}G_{2}
both in standard form such that
|{G^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle=\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})|{G^{\prime}_{1}}\rangle
.
If two vertices
u
and
v
are twins, i.e.
N_{G}(u)=N_{G}(v)
, then
X(\theta_{1})_{u}X(\theta_{2})_{v}|{G}\rangle=X(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})_{u}|{G}\rangle
. Indeed,
X(\theta)_{u}|{G}\rangle\!=\!e^{i\frac{\theta}{2}}(\cos(\frac{\theta}{2})|{G}\rangle+i\sin(\frac{\theta}{2})X_{u}|{G}\rangle)\!=\!e^{i\frac{\theta}{2}}(\cos(\frac{\theta}{2})|{G}\rangle+i\sin(\frac{\theta}{2})Z_{N_{G}(u)}|{G}\rangle)\allowbreak=X(\theta)_{v}|{G}\rangle
. Hence, without loss of generality, we suppose that if
u,v\in V_{X}
are twins, then at most one of the angles
\alpha_{u},\alpha_{v}
is non-zero.

Moreover, if a vertex
u
is a leaf, i.e. there exists a unique
v\in V
such that
u\sim_{G}v
, then
X(\theta)_{u}Z(-\theta)_{v}|{G}\rangle=|{G}\rangle
. Indeed,
X(\theta)_{u}|{G}\rangle=e^{i\frac{\theta}{2}}(\cos(\frac{\theta}{2})|{G}\rangle+i\sin(\frac{\theta}{2})Z_{v}|{G}\rangle)=Z(\theta)_{v}|{G}\rangle
. Hence, without loss of generality, we suppose that if
u\in V_{X}
is a leaf, then
\theta=0
.

If a vertex
u
is an isolated vertex,
X(\theta)_{u}|{G}\rangle=|{G}\rangle
. Hence, without loss of generality, we suppose that if a vertex
u\in V_{X}
is an isolated vertex, then
\alpha_{u}=0
.

Let us show that for every
u\in V_{X}
,
\alpha_{u}=0\bmod\pi/2^{|V_{Z}|-2+\delta(|V_{Z}|-2)}
.
If
|V_{Z}|\leqslant 1
, the proof is trivial as every vertex in
V_{X}
is a leaf or an isolated vertex. Else, we prove by induction over the size of a set
K\subseteq V_{Z}
that for every
u\in\Lambda_{G^{\prime}_{1}}^{V_{Z}\setminus K}\cap V_{X}
,
\alpha_{u}=0\bmod\pi/2^{|V_{Z}|-2+\delta(|V_{Z}|-2)}
.

•

If
K=\emptyset
: there is at most one vertex
u
in
\Lambda_{G^{\prime}_{1}}^{V_{Z}\setminus K}\cap V_{X}=\Lambda_{G^{\prime}_{1}}^{V_{Z}}\cap V_{X}
such that
\alpha_{u}
is non-zero. By
Lemma

12
,
\alpha_{u}=0\bmod\pi/2^{|V_{Z}|-2+\delta(|V_{Z}|-2)}
.

•

Else, there is at most one vertex
u
in
\left(\Lambda_{G^{\prime}_{1}}^{V_{Z}\setminus K}\setminus\bigcup_{K^{\prime}\varsubsetneq K}\Lambda_{G^{\prime}_{1}}^{V_{Z}\setminus K^{\prime}}\right)\cap V_{X}
such that
\alpha_{u}
is non-zero. For any vertex
v\in\Lambda_{G^{\prime}_{1}}^{V_{Z}\setminus K^{\prime}}\cap V_{X}
such that
K^{\prime}\varsubsetneq K
,
\alpha_{u}=0\bmod\pi/2^{|V_{Z}|-2+\delta(|V_{Z}|-2)}
by hypothesis of induction. If
|V_{Z}|-|K|=1
, then
u
is a leaf and thus
\alpha_{u}=0
. Else, by
Lemma

12
,
\sum_{w\in\Lambda_{G^{\prime}_{1}}^{V_{Z}\setminus K}\cap V_{X}}\alpha_{w}=0\bmod\dfrac{\pi}{2^{|V_{Z}|-2+\delta(|V_{Z}|-2)}}
, implying that
\alpha_{u}=0\bmod\dfrac{\pi}{2^{|V_{Z}|-2+\delta(|V_{Z}|-2)}}
.

A vertex
u\in V_{X}
such that for every
K\subseteq V_{Z}
,
u\notin\Lambda_{G^{\prime}_{1}}^{V_{Z}\setminus K}
, is an isolated vertex and thus
\alpha_{u}=0
. Hence, for every
u\in V_{X}
,
\alpha_{u}=0\bmod\dfrac{\pi}{2^{|V_{Z}|-2+\delta(|V_{Z}|-2)}}
. By
Lemma

12
, for every vertex
v\in V_{Z}
,
\beta_{v}=-\sum_{u\in N_{G^{\prime}_{1}}(v)\cap V_{X}}\alpha_{u}=0\bmod\dfrac{\pi}{2^{|V_{Z}|-2+\delta(|V_{Z}|-2)}}
.

By
Lemma

13
,
\bigotimes_{u\in V_{X}}X(\alpha_{u})\bigotimes_{v\in V_{Z}}Z(\beta_{v})
implements an
r
-local complementation where
r\leqslant|V_{Z}|-1
.
∎

Remarkably,
Theorem

6
(along with
Proposition

41
) implies that for graph states, LU-equivalence reduces to equivalence up to local unitaries in the Clifford hierarchy. This has been hinted previously in
[
41
]
, and similar restrictions occur in the symmetries of graph states
[
78
]
.

In
Chapter

5
we strengthen
Theorem

6
by proving that
r
can be actually be bounded by
log_{2}(n)+O(1)
. This bound reveals very useful for algorithmic purposes.

4.7
An infinite strict hierarchy of local equivalences

Two graphs related by
r
-local complementations are also related by
(r+1)
-local complementations. This can be seen graphically as a direct consequence of
Proposition

45
, or using graph states through
Theorem

5
, as two LC
r
-equivalent states are obviously LC
r+1
-equivalent. The known counter examples to the LU-LC conjecture introduced in
[
37
,
43
]
are actually LC
2
-equivalent graphs that are not LC-equivalent. There was however no known examples of graphs that are LC
3
-equivalent but not LC
2
-equivalent, and more generally no known examples of graphs that are LC
r+1
-equivalent but not LC
r
-equivalent for
r\geqslant 2
. We introduce such examples in this section, by showing that for any
r
there exist pairs of graphs that are LC
r+1
-equivalent but not LC
r
-equivalent, leading to an infinite hierarchy of generalized local equivalences. Our proof is constructive.

We introduce a family of bipartite graphs
C_{t,k}
parametrized by two integers
t
and
k
. This family is a variant of the (bipartite) Kneser graphs and uniform subset graphs. The graph
C_{t,k}
is bipartite: the first independent set of vertices corresponds to the integers from
1
to
t
, and the second independent set is composed of all the subsets of
[1,t]
of size
k
(we note
\binom{[1,t]}{k}
). There is an edge between an integer
u\in[1,t]
and a subset
A\subseteq[1,t]
of size
k
if and only if
u\in A
:

Definition 20
.

For any
k\geqslant 1
and
t\geqslant k
two integers, let
C_{t,k}=(V,E)
be a bipartite graph defined as:
V=[1,t]\cup\binom{[1,t]}{k}
and
E=\{(u,A)\in[1,t]\times\binom{[1,t]}{k}\penalty\ |\penalty\ u\in A\}
.

We introduce a second family of graphs
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
, defined similarly to
C_{t,k}
, the independent set
[1,t]
being replaced by a clique:

Definition 21
.

For any
k\geqslant 1
and
t\geqslant k
two integers, let
C^{\prime}_{t,k}=(V,E)
be a graph defined as:
V=[1,t]\cup\binom{[1,t]}{k}
and
E=\{(u,A)\in[1,t]\times\binom{[1,t]}{k}\penalty\ |\penalty\ u\in A\}\cup\{(u,v)\in[1,t]\times[1,t]\penalty\ |\penalty\ u\neq v\}
.

Examples of such graphs with parameters
t=4
and
k=2
are given in Figure
4.5
.

1
2
3
4
\{1,2\}
\{1,3\}
\{1,4\}
\{2,3\}
\{2,4\}
\{3,4\}

1
2
3
4
\{1,2\}
\{1,3\}
\{1,4\}
\{2,3\}
\{2,4\}
\{3,4\}

Figure 4.5
:
(Left) The graph
C_{4,2}
. (Right) The graph
C^{\prime}_{4,2}
.

For some parameters
t
and
k
,
C_{t,k}
and
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
are in standard form with respect to the maximal MLS cover
\mathcal{M}_{max}=\{L\subseteq V\penalty\ |\penalty\ L\text{ is a minimal local set}\}
.

Proposition 51
.

For any odd
k\geqslant 3
,
t\geqslant k+2
,
C_{t,k}
and
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
are in standard form, with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{max}
, for any ordering such that, for any
u\in\binom{[1,t]}{k}
and for any
v\in[1,t]
,
u<v
. More precisely the vertices in
\binom{[1,t]}{k}
are of type X and the vertices in
[1,t]
are of type Z.

Proof.

Fix two integers
k
and
t
such that
k\geqslant 3
is odd and
t\geqslant k+2
.
First, let us prove by contradiction that a minimal local set cannot be generated by a set containing a vertex in
[1,t]
. Suppose that a set
D\subseteq V
such that
D\cap[1,t]\neq\emptyset
, generates a minimal local set
L
in
C_{t,k}
. By symmetry, we suppose without loss of generality that
D\cap[1,t]=[1,m]
for some
m\in[1,t]
. Let us exhibit a local set strictly contained in
L
, implying a contradiction with the fact that
L
is a minimal local set:

•

If
m=1
i.e.
D\cap[1,t]=\{1\}
:
L
contains every vertex of the form
\{\{1\}\cup x\}
for some
x\in\binom{[2,t]}{k-1}
. Let
D^{\prime}=\{\{1\}\cup x\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in\binom{[2,k+1]}{k-1}\}
.
Odd_{C_{t,k}}(D^{\prime})=\{1\}
, so
D^{\prime}\cup Odd_{C_{t,k}}(D^{\prime})
is a local set contained strictly in
L
(
L
contains other vertices, e.g. the vertex
\{1,4,5,\cdots,k+2\}
).

•

If
m\geqslant 2
: let
m_{\text{odd}}
be the minimum between
k
and the largest odd integer strictly less than
m
. Let
D^{\prime}=\{\{1,\cdots,m_{\text{odd}},m+1,\cdots,m+k-m_{\text{odd}}\},\{2,\cdots,m_{\text{odd}}+1,m+1,\cdots,m+k-m_{\text{odd}}\}\}
.
Odd_{C_{t,k}}(D^{\prime})=\{1,m_{\text{odd}}+1\}
, so
D^{\prime}\cup Odd_{C_{t,k}}(D^{\prime})
is a local set contained strictly in
L
(
L
contains other vertices, e.g. the vertex
\{1,3,\cdots,m_{\text{odd}}+1,m+1,\cdots,m+k-m_{\text{odd}}\}
).

Thus, every minimal local set is generated by a set in
\binom{[1,t]}{k}
. As every vertex is covered by a minimal local set by
Theorem

2
, it follows that each vertex in
\binom{[1,t]}{k}
is of type X and each vertex in
[1,t]
is of type Z. The proof is the same for
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
as
C_{t,k}
and
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
differ only by edges between vertices of
[1,t]
thus for any set
D\subseteq V
,
(D\cup Odd_{C_{t,k}}(D))\cap\binom{[1,t]}{k}=(D\cup Odd_{C^{\prime}_{t,k}}(D))\cap\binom{[1,t]}{k}
.
∎

The following proposition gives a sufficient condition on
t
and
k
for the LC
r
-equivalence of
C_{t,k}
and
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
.

Proposition 52
.

For any
t\geqslant k\geqslant 1
,
C_{t,k}
and
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
are LC
r
-equivalent if

\binom{t-2}{k-2}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ and\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ }\forall i\in[1,r-1]\text{, \penalty\ \penalty\ }\binom{t-i-2}{k-i-2}=0\bmod 2^{r-i}

Proof.

C_{t,k}
and
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
are related by an
r
-local complementation over the set
\binom{[1,t]}{k}
(which can be seen as the multiset where each vertex of
\binom{[1,t]}{k}
appears once). Let
K\subseteq[1,t]
of size
k^{\prime}+2
.
\binom{[1,t]}{k}\bullet\Lambda_{C_{t,k}}^{K}=\left|\left\{x\in\binom{[1,t]}{k}\penalty\ |\penalty\ K\subseteq x\right\}\right|=\binom{t-k^{\prime}-2}{k-k^{\prime}-2}
is a multiple of
2^{r-k^{\prime}-\delta(k^{\prime})}
by hypothesis. Thus,
\binom{[1,t]}{k}
is
r
-incident. Besides, for any
u,v\in[1,t]
,
\binom{[1,t]}{k}\bullet\Lambda_{C_{t,k}}^{u,v}=\binom{t-2}{k-2}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}
by hypothesis. Thus,
C_{t,k}\star^{r}\binom{[1,t]}{k}=C^{\prime}_{t,k}
.
∎

The following proposition provides a sufficient condition on
t
and
k
for the non LC
r
-equivalence of
C_{t,k}
and
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
.

Proposition 53
.

For any odd
k\geqslant 3
,
t\geqslant k+2
,
C_{t,k}
and
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
are not LC
r
-equivalent if
\binom{t}{2}
is odd and
\binom{k}{2}=0\bmod 2^{r}
.

Proof.

Let us suppose by contradiction that
C_{t,k}
and
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
are LC
r
-equivalent.
By
Corollary

9
, they are related by a single
r
-local complementation over vertices in
\binom{[1,t]}{k}
. Let
S
be the multiset in
\binom{[1,t]}{k}
such that
C_{t,k}\star^{r}S=C^{\prime}_{t,k}
. For each
u,v\in[1,t]
,
S\bullet\Lambda_{C_{t,k}}^{u,v}=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{C_{t,k}}^{u,v}}S(x)=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}
. Summing over all pairs
u,v\in[1,t]
, and, as by hypothesis
\binom{t}{2}
is odd,
\sum_{u,v\in[1,t]}S\bullet\Lambda_{C_{t,k}}^{u,v}=\binom{k}{2}\sum_{x\in\binom{[1,t]}{k}}S(x)=\binom{t}{2}2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}
. The first part of the equation is true because
\sum_{u,v\in[1,t]}S\bullet\Lambda_{C_{t,k}}^{u,v}=\sum_{u,v\in[1,t]}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{C_{t,k}}^{u,v}}S(x)=\sum_{x\in\binom{[1,t]}{k}}\sum_{u,v\in x}S(x)
. This leads to a contradiction as
\binom{k}{2}=0\bmod 2^{r}
by hypothesis.
∎

It remains to find, for any
r
, parameters
t
and
k
such that the corresponding graphs are LC
r
-equivalent but not LC
r-1
-equivalent. Such parameters exist.

Theorem 7
.

For any
r\geqslant 2
,
|{C_{t,k}}\rangle
and
|{C^{\prime}_{t,k}}\rangle
are LC
r
-equivalent but not LC
r-1
-equivalent when
k=2^{r}+1
and
t=2^{r}+2^{\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor+1}-1
.

We begin by proving the following technical lemma.

Lemma 15
.

Given any integers
s\leqslant m
,
\binom{m}{s}=2^{w(s)+w(m-s)-w(m)}q
where
q
is an odd integer and
w(i)
denotes the Hamming weight of the integer
i
.

Proof.

We prove by induction that for any
n\geqslant 0
,
n!=2^{n-w(n)}q
where
q
is an odd integer. The property is trivially true for
n=0
. Given
n>0
, by induction hypothesis, there exists
q=1\bmod 2
s.t.
n!=n(n-1)!=n2^{n-1-w(n-1)}q=2^{k}q^{\prime}2^{n-1-w(n-1)}q=2^{n-1+k-w(n-1)}qq^{\prime}
, where
n=2^{k}q^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}=1\bmod 2
. Notice that
w(n)=w(q^{\prime})
, as the binary notation of
n
is nothing but the binary notation of
q^{\prime}
concatenated with
k
zeros. Moreover,
w(n-1)=w(q^{\prime})-1+k=w(n)-1+k
as
n-1=2^{k}(q^{\prime}-1)+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}2^{j}
. Therefore,
n!=2^{n-w(n)}qq^{\prime}
.

As a consequence there exist three odd integers
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
and
q_{3}
such that

\binom{m}{s}=\dfrac{m!}{s!(m-s)!}=\dfrac{2^{m-w(m)}q_{1}}{2^{s-w(s)}q_{2}2^{m-s-w(m-s)}q_{3}}=2^{w(s)+w(m-s)-w(m)}\dfrac{q_{1}}{q_{2}q_{3}}\qed

Proof of
Theorem

7
.

Proposition

52
along with
Proposition

53
imply that for any odd
k\geqslant 3
and
t\geqslant k+2
,
C_{t,k}
and
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
are LC
r
-equivalent but not LC
r-1
-equivalent if

\begin{split}\binom{t}{2}&=1\bmod 2\\
\binom{k}{2}&=0\bmod 2^{r-1}\\
\binom{t-2}{k-2}&=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}\\
\forall i\in[1,r-1]\text{, \penalty\ \penalty\ }\binom{t-i-2}{k-i-2}&=0\bmod 2^{r-i}\end{split}

By
Lemma

15
, it is equivalent to prove the following equations:

\begin{split}&w(t)-w(t-2)=1\\
&w(k-2)-w(k)\geqslant r-2\\
&w(k-2)+w(t-k)-w(t-2)=r-1\\
\forall i\in[1,r-1]\text{, \penalty\ \penalty\ }&w(k-2-i)+w(t-k)-w(t-2-i)\geqslant r-i\end{split}

Let us prove that these equations are satisfied for
k=2^{r}+1
and
t=2^{r}+2^{\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor+1}-1
. We will make use of the following property of the hamming weight: if
i\leqslant 2^{z}-1
, then
w(2^{z}-1-i)=z-w(i)
.

•

w(t)=\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor+2
,
w(t-2)=\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor+1
, thus
w(t)-w(t-2)=1
.

•

w(k)=2
and
w(k-2)=r
, thus
w(k-2)-w(k)=r-2
.

•

w(t-k)=w(2^{\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor+1}-2)=\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor
and
w(t-2)=w(2^{r}+2^{\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor+1}-1-2)=\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor+2-w(2)=\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor+1
. Thus,
w(k-2)+w(t-k)-w(t-2)=r+\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor-\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor-1=r-1
.

•

Let
i\in[1,r-1]
.
w(k-2-i)=w(2^{r}-1-i)=r-w(i)
and
w(t-2-i)=w(2^{r}+2^{\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor+1}-1-(i+2))=\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor+2-w(i+2)
. Thus,
w(k-2-i)+w(t-k)-w(t-2-i)=r-w(i)+\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor-\lfloor\log_{2}(r)\rfloor-2+w(i+2)=r-(w(i)-w(i+2)+2)
. It is easy to check that
w(i)-w(i+2)+2\leqslant i
. Thus,
w(k-2-i)+w(t-k)-w(t-2-i)\geqslant r-i
.

Hence,
C_{t,k}
and
C^{\prime}_{t,k}
are LC
r
-equivalent but not LC
r-1
-equivalent. By
Theorem

5
,
|{C_{t,k}}\rangle
and
|{C^{\prime}_{t,k}}\rangle
are LC
r
-equivalent but not LC
r-1
-equivalent.
∎

Remark 8
.

For the case
r=2
, we obtain the pair
(C_{7,5},C^{\prime}_{7,5})
, which is the 28-vertex counter-example to the LU-LC conjecture from
[
43
]
. This is an example of graphs that are LC
2
-equivalent but not LC-equivalent.

While Ji et al. proved that there exist pairs of graphs that are LU-equivalent but not LC-equivalent
[
37
]
, we showed a finer result – the existence of an infinite strict hierarchy of graph states equivalence between LC- and LU-equivalence. There exist LC
2
-equivalent graphs that are not LC-equivalent, LC
3
-equivalent graphs that are not LC
2
-equivalent, and so on. On the other end, for any integer
r
, there exist LU-equivalent graphs that are not LC
r
-equivalent.

Chapter 5
A quasi-polynomial algorithm for LU-equivalence

In this chapter, we address the problem of deciding whether two given graphs are LU-equivalent. Additionally, we consider a variant of this problem consisting in deciding whether two graphs are LC
r
-equivalent for a fixed
r
. Since LU-equivalent graphs are necessarily LC
r
-equivalent for some
r
(see
Theorem

6
), the difference lies in whether the level
r
is fixed or not.

Thanks to
Theorem

5
, if
G_{1}
is LC
r
-equivalent to
G_{2}
, there exists a single
r
-local complementation, together with usual local complementations, that transforms
G_{1}
into
G_{2}
. We introduce an algorithm that builds such a sequence of generalized local complementations, in essentially four stages:

(i)

Both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are turned in standard forms
G_{1}^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}_{2}
by means of (usual) local complementations.

(ii)

We then focus on the single
r
-local complementation: all the possible actions of a single
r
-local complementation over
G^{\prime}_{1}
are described as a vector space, for which we compute a basis
\mathcal{B}
.

(iii)

It remains to find, if it exists, the
r
-local complementation to apply on
G^{\prime}_{1}
that leads to
G^{\prime}_{2}
up to some additional usual local complementations. With an appropriate construction depending on
G_{1}^{\prime}
,
G^{\prime}_{2}
and
\mathcal{B}
, we reduce this problem to deciding whether two graphs are LC-equivalent under some additional requirements on the sequence of local complementations to apply. These requirements can be expressed as linear constraints.

(iv)

Finally, to find such a sequence of local complementations, we apply a variant of Bouchet’s algorithm, generalized to accommodate the additional linear constraints.

Stages
(i)
,
(iii)
and
(iv)
can be performed in polynomial time in the order
n
of the graphs. Stage
(ii)
has essentially an
O(n^{r})
time complexity, thus deciding LC
r
-equivalence for a fixed
r
can be done in polynomial time. Regarding LU-equivalence,
Theorem

6
implies
r\leqslant n
. We improve this upperbound and show that
r
is at most logarithmic in
n
, leading to a quasi-polynomial time algorithm for LU-equivalence.

This chapter is dedicated to the description of the algorithm, its correctness and complexity, beginning with the generalization of Bouchet’s algorithm to decide, in polynomial time, LC-equivalence with additional constraints.

5.1
Extending Bouchet’s algorithm for LC-equivalence

In this section, we consider an extension of Bouchet’s algorithm (see
Section

2.5.3
) where an additional set of linear constraints on
A,B,C
and
D
is added as input of the problem. Such additional linear equations can reflect constraints on the applied local complementations, e.g. deciding whether two graphs are LC-equivalent under the additional constraint that all local complementations are applied on a fixed set
V_{0}
of vertices (see
Example

1
).

Recall that Bouchet’s algorithm for deciding LC-equivalence of
G
and
G^{\prime}
consists in solving for sets
A,B,C,D\subseteq V
such that

(i)

\forall u,v\in V
,

|B\cap N_{G}(u)\cap N_{G^{\prime}}(v)|+|A\cap N_{G}(u)\cap\{v\}|+|D\cap\{u\}\cap N_{G^{\prime}}(v)|+|C\cap\{u\}\cap\{v\}|=0\bmod 2

(ii)

(A\cap D)\penalty\ \Delta\penalty\ (B\cap C)=V

While solving linear equations is easy, equation
(ii)
is not linear.
Bouchet showed that a set of solutions
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
that satisfies both
(i)
and
(ii)
, is either small or it contains an affine subspace of
\mathcal{S}
of small co-dimension. In the latter case, the entire set
\mathcal{S}
is actually an affine space except for some particular cases that can be avoided by assuming that the graphs contain vertices of even degree. We extend this result as follows:

Lemma 16
.

Given
G
,
G^{\prime}
two connected graphs with an even-degree vertex, and a set
L
of linear constraints on
V^{4}
, then either the set
\mathcal{S}_{L}
of solutions to both
L
and
(i)
is of dimension at most
4
, or the set
\mathcal{C}_{L}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{L}
that additionally satisfies
(ii)
is either empty or an affine subspace of
\mathcal{S}_{L}
of codimension at most
2
.

Proof.

It is enough to consider the case
\dim(\mathcal{S}_{L})>4
and
\mathcal{C}_{L}\neq\emptyset
. The set
\mathcal{S}
of solutions to
(i)
contains
\mathcal{S}_{L}
, so
\dim(\mathcal{S})>4
. According to
[
50
]
,
\mathcal{C}
, the solutions to
(i)
and
(ii)
, is an affine subspace of
\mathcal{S}
of codimension at most
2
. For any
a\in\mathcal{C}_{L}
,
\mathcal{C}=a+\mathcal{N}
where
\mathcal{N}
is a subvector space of
\mathcal{S}
. Notice that
\mathcal{C}_{L}=\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{S}_{L}=a+\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{S}_{L}
. We have
\dim(\mathcal{C}_{L})=\dim(\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{S}_{L})=\dim(\mathcal{N})+\dim(\mathcal{S}_{L})-\dim(\mathcal{N}+\mathcal{S}_{L})\geqslant\dim(\mathcal{N})+\dim(\mathcal{S}_{L})-\dim(\mathcal{S})\geqslant\dim(\mathcal{S}_{L})-2
as
\mathcal{N}
is of codimension at most
2
in
\mathcal{S}
.
∎

Remark 9
.

Lemma

16
holds actually for any graph that is not in the so-called "Class
\alpha
" of graphs with only odd-degree vertices together with a few additional properties
1
1

1

(a) any pair of unadjacent vertices should have an even number of common neighbors; (b) for any cycle
C
, the number of triangles having an edge in
C
is equal to the size of
C
modulo
2
.
. When the graphs are in "Class
\alpha
", and in the absence of additional constraints, Bouchet proved that there is at most
2
solutions in
\mathcal{C}
that do not belong to the affine subspace of small codimension, and these two solutions can be easily computed (see
[
50
]
, section 7). We leave as an open question the description of the set of solutions for graphs in "Class
\alpha
", in particular when the two particular solutions pointed out by Bouchet do not satisfy
L
, the set of additional constraints.

From an algorithmic point of view,
Lemma

16
leads to a straightforward generalization of Bouchet’s algorithm to efficiently decide LC-equivalence of graphs, under a set of additional linear constraints:

Proposition 54
.

Given
G
,
G^{\prime}
two connected graphs of order
n
with an even-degree vertex, and a set
L
of
\ell
linear constraints on
V^{4}
, one can compute a solution to both
(i)
,
(ii)
and
L
when it exists, or claim there is no solution, in runtime
O((n^{2}+\ell)n^{2})
.

Proof.

A Gaussian elimination can be used to compute a basis
\mathcal{B}=\{S_{0},\ldots,S_{k}\}
of
\mathcal{S}_{L}
, with
k<n
, in
O((n^{2}+\ell)n^{2})
operations as there are
n^{2}
equations in
(ii)
. If the dimension of
\mathcal{S}_{L}
is at most
4
(so
|\mathcal{S}_{L}|\leqslant 16
), we check in
O(n)
operations, for each element of
\mathcal{S}_{L}
whether equation
(ii)
is satisfied. Otherwise, when
\dim(\mathcal{S}_{L})>4
, if
\mathcal{C}_{L}
is non-empty, at least one element of
\mathcal{C}_{L}
is the sum
S_{i}+S_{j}
of two elements of
\mathcal{B}
(see Lemma 4.4 in
[
50
]
). For each of the
O(n^{2})
candidates we check whether condition
(ii)
is satisfied. If no solution is found, it implies that
\mathcal{C}_{L}=\emptyset
.
∎

As the algorithm described in the proof of
Proposition

20
translates a solution to
(i)
and
(ii)
, into a sequence of local complementations relating two graphs, some constraints on the sequence of local complementations may be encoded as additional linear constraints. We give a fairly simple example below. A more intricate example is presented in
Lemma

19
(in
Section

5.2
).

Example 1
.

Let
G
,
G^{\prime}
be two connected graphs of order
n
with an even-degree vertex, and
V_{0}
a set of vertices. One can decide in runtime
O(n^{4})
whether there exists a sequence of (possibly repeating) vertices
a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\in V_{0}
such that
G^{\prime}=G\star a_{1}\star\cdots\star a_{m}
. Roughly speaking, the idea is to consider the linear constraint
b_{u}=0
(i.e.
u\in\overline{B}
) for any
u\notin V_{0}
, to reflect the constraints that local complementations should not be applied outside
V_{0}
.

From a graph state point of view,
Proposition

54
provides an efficient algorithm to decide whether two graph states are LC-equivalent under some constraints on the Clifford operators. Notice that such constraints are expressible as a linear equation through the correspondence given in
Proposition

23
. We give below a non-exhaustive family of constraints expressible as linear equations (in the following,
k
denotes an integer).

•

u\notin B
:
U_{u}
is
Z(k\pi/2)
up to Pauli;

•

u\notin C
:
U_{u}
is
X(k\pi/2)
up to Pauli;

•

u\notin A
:
U_{u}
is
Z(k\pi/2)H
up to Pauli;

•

u\notin D
:
U_{u}
is
X(k\pi/2)H
up to Pauli;

•

u\notin\overline{B}\cap\overline{C}
:
U_{u}
is a Pauli;

•

u\notin\overline{A}\cap\overline{D}
:
U_{u}
is
H
up to a Pauli;

•

u\in A
iff
u\in D
:
U_{u}
is I,
X(\pi/2)
,
Z(\pi/2)
or
H
up to Pauli, i.e.
u^{2}
is a Pauli;

•

u\in A
iff
u\in B
:
U_{u}
is
X(\pi/2)
or
X(k\pi/2)H
up to Pauli;

•

u\in A
iff
v\in A
,
u\in B
iff
v\in B
,
u\in C
iff
v\in C
,
u\in D
iff
v\in D
:
U_{u}=U_{v}
up to Pauli.

5.2
An algorithm to recognize LC
r
-equivalent graph states

We are now ready to describe the algorithm that recognizes two LC
r
-equivalent graphs. We consider a level
r\geqslant 1
, and two graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
of order
n
, defined on the same vertex set
V
. Following
Lemma

9
, assume, without loss of generality, that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are both in standard form with respect to the same MLS cover. Then, it is valid (see
Lemma

10
) to define
V_{X},V_{Z}\subseteq V
, the sets of vertices respectively of type X and Z with respect to the MLS cover. Also,
V_{X}
is independent and each vertex in
V_{X}
has the same neighborhood in both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
. According to
Lemma

14
, if
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are LC
r
-equivalent then there is a single
r
-local complementation over vertices of
V_{X}
together with a series of local complementations on vertices of type
\bot
that transform
G_{1}
into
G_{2}
. We first focus on the single
r
-local complementation (which commutes with the local complementations on vertices of type
\bot
, as vertices of type X and vertices of type
\bot
share no edge) and thus consider all the possible graphs that can be reached from
G_{1}
by mean of a single
r
-local complementation over vertices of
V_{X}
.
Notice that such an
r
-local complementation only toggles edges for which both endpoints are in
V_{Z}
. Given a multiset
S
, the edges toggled in
G_{1}\star^{r}S
can be represented by a vector
\omega^{(S)}\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}^{\{u,v\in V_{Z}\penalty\ |\penalty\ u\neq v\}}
such that for any
u,v\in V_{Z}
,
\omega^{(S)}_{u,v}=u\sim_{G_{1}}v\penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ u\sim_{G_{1}\star^{r}S}v
. The actions of all the possible
r
-local complementations on
G_{1}
can thus be described as the set
\Omega=\{\omega^{(S)}|\text{$S$ is an $r$-incident multiset of vertices of type X}\}
.

Lemma 17
.

\Omega
is a vector space and a basis
\mathcal{B}
of
\Omega
can be computed in runtime
O(rn^{r+2.38})
.

Proof.

A multiset
S
of vertices of type X can be represented as a vector in
({\mathbb{Z}/2^{r}\mathbb{Z}})^{V_{X}}
which entries are
S(u)\bmod 2^{r}
, as the multiplicity can be considered modulo
2^{r}
in the context of an
r
-local complementation. Let
\Sigma
be the subset of
({\mathbb{Z}/2^{r}\mathbb{Z}})^{V_{X}}
that corresponds to all
r
-incident independent multisets of vertices in
V_{X}
.
\Sigma
is a vector space since the property of
r
-incidence is preserved under the addition of two vectors, as
V_{X}
is independent.
\Sigma
is actually the space of solutions to a set of
O(n^{r+1})
equations
2
2

2

There are precisely
\binom{|V_{X}|}{r+1}+\binom{|V_{X}|}{r}+\cdots+\binom{|V_{X}|}{3}+\binom{|V_{X}|}{2}
equations.
given by the conditions of
r
-incidence. With some multiplications by powers of 2, all these equations are expressible as equations modulo
2^{r}
. For every set
K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
of size between
2
and
r+1
, the corresponding equation is

\sum_{u\in\Lambda_{G_{1}}^{K}}2^{|K|-2+\delta(|K|-2)}S(u)=0\bmod 2^{r}

Notice that if
r=1
, the space of solutions is the entire space
({\mathbb{Z}/2^{r}\mathbb{Z}})^{V_{X}}
, as there are no incidence constraints on usual local complementations. Summing up, to compute a basis of
\Sigma
, we solve a system of
O(n^{r+1})
equations modulo
2^{r}
with
O(n)
variables. One can obtain a generating set
\{S_{1},S_{2},...,S_{t}\}
of
\Sigma
of size
t\leqslant n
in
O(rn^{r+2.38})
basic operations using an algorithm based on the Howell transform
[
79
]
.

Let
f
be the function that associates with each element
S\in\Sigma
, its action
f(S)\in\Omega
on the edges: for any
u,v\in V_{Z}
,
f(S)_{u,v}=u\sim_{G_{1}}v\penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ u\sim_{G_{1}\star^{r}S}v
. Notice that
f
is linear, as for any
S,S^{\prime}\in\Sigma
:

\displaystyle f(S+S^{\prime})

\displaystyle=u\sim_{G_{1}}v\penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ u\sim_{G_{1}\star^{r}(S+S^{\prime})}v=u\sim_{G_{1}}v\penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ u\sim_{(G_{1}\star^{r}S)\star^{r}S^{\prime}}v

\displaystyle=u\sim_{G_{1}}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ u\sim_{G_{1}\star^{r}S}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(S^{\prime}\bullet\Lambda_{G_{1}\star^{r}S}^{u,v}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}\right)

\displaystyle=u\sim_{G_{1}}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ u\sim_{G_{1}\star^{r}S}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(S^{\prime}\bullet\Lambda_{G_{1}}^{u,v}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}\right)

\displaystyle=u\sim_{G_{1}\star^{r}S}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ u\sim_{G_{1}\star^{r}S^{\prime}}v

\displaystyle=u\sim_{G_{1}}v\penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ u\sim_{G_{1}\star^{r}S}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ u\sim_{G_{1}}v\penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ u\sim_{G_{1}\star^{r}S^{\prime}}v=f(S)+f(S^{\prime})

This directly implies that
\Omega
is a vector space: if
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
, by definition there exist
S,S^{\prime}\in\Sigma
such that
f(S)=\omega
and
f(S^{\prime})=\omega
, moreover
\omega+\omega^{\prime}=f(S)+f(S^{\prime})=f(S+S^{\prime})\in\Omega
. Also, let us prove that
\Omega
is generated by
\{f(S_{1}),f(S_{2}),...,f(S_{t})\}
. Take a vector
\tilde{\omega}\in\Omega
, by definition there exists
\tilde{S}\in\Sigma
such that
\tilde{\omega}=f(\tilde{S})
.
\tilde{S}
can be expressed as a linear combination of vectors from the generating set, i.e.
\tilde{S}=\sum_{i\in[1,t]}a_{i}S_{i}
where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}/2^{r}\mathbb{Z}
. Then, for any
u,v\in V_{Z}
,
(\tilde{\omega})_{u,v}=f\left(\sum_{i\in[1,t]}a_{i}S_{i}\right)_{u,v}=\sum_{i\in[1,t]}a^{\prime}_{i}f(S_{i})_{u,v}
by linearity of
f
, where
a^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
such that
a^{\prime}_{i}=a_{i}\bmod 2
when
a_{i}
and
a^{\prime}_{i}
are viewed as integers. In other words,
\tilde{w}=\sum_{i\in[1,t]}a^{\prime}_{i}f(S_{i})
, implying that
\{f(S_{1}),f(S_{2}),...,f(S_{t})\}
is a generating set of
\Omega
. Using Gaussian elimination, one can easily obtain a basis
\mathcal{B}
of
\Omega
from
\{f(S_{1}),f(S_{2}),...,f(S_{t})\}
.
∎

Thanks to the exhaustive description of all possible
r
-local complementations on
G_{1}
, we are now ready to reduce LC
r
-equivalence to LC-equivalence with some additional constraint. We denote by
G_{1}^{\#}
(resp.
G_{2}^{\#}
) the graph obtained from
G_{1}
(resp.
G_{2}
) by the following procedure. First, remove the vertices of
V_{X}
. Then, for each vector
\omega\in\mathcal{B}
, for each
u,v\in V_{Z}
such that
\omega_{u,v}=1
, add a vertex adjacent only to
u
and
v
and call it
p_{u,v}^{\omega}
, and let
\mathcal{P}^{\omega}=\{p_{u,v}^{\omega}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \omega_{u,v}=1\}
. In the following, we refer to the vertices added by this procedure as "new vertices".

Lemma 18
.

G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are LC
r
-equivalent if and only if there exists a sequence of (possibly repeating) vertices
a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}
such that
G^{\#}_{2}=G^{\#}_{1}\star a_{1}\star\cdots\star a_{m}
satisfying the following additional constraints:

•

the sequence contains no vertex of
V_{Z}
;

•

for each
\omega\in\mathcal{B}
, either the sequence contains every vertex of
\mathcal{P}^{\omega}
exactly once, or it contains none.

Proof.

Suppose
G_{1}=_{LC_{r}}G_{2}
. According to
Lemma

14
,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are related by a single
r
-local complementation over a multiset
S
whose support lies in
V_{X}
, along with a sequence of local complementations on the vertices of
V\setminus(V_{X}\cup V_{Z})
. We note
G_{2}=G_{1}\star^{r}S\star u_{1}\star\cdots u_{k}
. The edges toggled by an
r
-local complementation over
S
are described by an element
\omega\in\Omega
. Let us decompose
\omega
as a linear combination of basis vectors of
\Omega
:
\omega=\omega_{1}+\cdots+\omega_{t}
, where each
\omega_{i}\in\mathcal{B}
. In
G^{\#}_{1}
and
G^{\#}_{2}
, let
V_{\omega}=\bigcup_{i\in[1,t]}\mathcal{P}^{\omega_{i}}
.
Then,
G^{\#}_{2}=G^{\#}_{1}\star^{1}V_{\omega}\star u_{1}\star\cdots u_{k}
. Note that the 1-local complementation over the set
V_{\omega}
corresponds to the composition of local complementations on each element of
V_{\omega}
. Thus,
G_{1}
is mapped to
G_{2}
by a sequence of local complementations that satisfy the additional constraints.

Conversely, suppose there exists a sequence of (possibly repeating) vertices
a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}
such that
G^{\#}_{2}=G^{\#}_{1}\star a_{1}\star\cdots\star a_{m}
satisfying the additional constraints. As local complementations on new vertices commute with each other and with local complementations on vertices of
V\setminus(V_{X}\cup V_{Z})
, one can take apart the vertices of the sequence among the new vertices, so there exist a set
V_{0}
of new vertices and vertices
u_{i}
in
V\setminus(V_{X}\cup V_{Z})
such that
G^{\#}_{2}=G^{\#}_{1}\star^{1}V_{0}\star u_{1}\star\cdots u_{k}
. The additional constraints imply that
V_{0}
is a union of sets of vertices corresponding respectively to some elements
\omega_{i}\in\mathcal{B}
. Let
\omega\in\Omega
be the sum of these elements. By construction, there exists a multiset
S
in the original graphs whose action is described by
\omega
, implying
G_{2}=G_{1}\star^{r}S\star u_{1}\star\cdots u_{k}
. Thus,
G_{1}=_{LC_{r}}G_{2}
.
∎

There exists an efficient algorithm that decides whether two graphs are LC-equivalent with such additional constraints using our generalization of Bouchet’s algorithm.

Lemma 19
.

Deciding whether there exists a sequence of (possibly repeating) vertices
a_{1},\!\cdots\!,\!a_{m}
such that
G^{\#}_{2}=G^{\#}_{1}\star a_{1}\star\cdots\star a_{m}
, satisfying the additional constraints described in
Lemma

18
, can be done in runtime
O(n^{4})
.

Proof.

If the vector space
\Omega
is of dimension zero (i.e.
\Omega
only contains the null vector), then there is no additional constraint, thus one can apply the usual Bouchet algorithm that decides LC-equivalence of graphs.

If
\Omega
is not of dimension zero, then
G^{\#}_{1}
and
G^{\#}_{2}
both have at least one even-degree vertex (since every "new vertex" is of degree 2). Using the notations of
Section

2.5.3
, let us define the following linear constraints on
V^{4}
:
\forall u\in V_{Z}
,
\forall\omega\in\mathcal{B}
,
\forall v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\omega}
,

•

u\in\overline{B}
;

•

v\in\overline{C}
;

•

v\in B
if and only if
v^{\prime}\in B
.

According to
Proposition

54
, a solution to the system of equations composed of
(i)
,
(ii)
(see
Proposition

20
) and the additional linear constraints can be computed in runtime
O(n^{4})
when it exists. Following the algorithm in the proof of
Proposition

20
, such a solution yields a sequence of local complementations satisfying the additional constraints described in
Lemma

18
. Conversely, such a sequence of local complementations can be converted into a valid solution to the system of equations.
∎

Summing up, we have an algorithm that decides, for a fixed level
r
, the LC
r
-equivalence of graphs in polynomial runtime.

Theorem 8
.

There exists an algorithm that decides if two graph states are LC
r
-equivalent with runtime
O(rn^{r+2.38}+n^{6.38})
, where
n
is the number of qubits.

The algorithm reads as follows:

1.

Put
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
in standard form with respect to the same MLS cover if possible, otherwise output NO.

2.

Check whether each vertex has the same type in
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, and whether every vertex of type X has the same neighborhood in both graphs, otherwise output NO.

3.

Compute a basis of the vector space
\Omega
.

4.

Compute the graphs
{G}^{\#}_{1}
and
{G}^{\#}_{2}
.

5.

Decide whether
{G}^{\#}_{1}
and
{G}^{\#}_{2}
are LC-equivalent with the additional constraints described in
Lemma

18
. Output YES if this is the case, NO otherwise.

Notice that the algorithm is exponential in
r
, in particular it does not provide an efficient algorithm to decide LU-equivalence of graph states. To address this issue, we provide in the next section some upper bounds on the level of a generalized local complementation.

5.3
Bounds for generalized local complementation

In this section, we prove an upper bound on the level of a valid generalized local complementation: roughly speaking we show that if
G\star^{r}S
is valid then
r
is at most logarithmic in the order
n
of the graph
G
. This bound is however not true in general as
whenever
G\star^{r}S
is valid, we have
G\star^{r}S=G\star^{r+1}(2S)
, according to
Proposition

45
.
To avoid these pathological cases, we thus focus on genuine
r
-incident independent multisets:

Definition 22
.

Given a graph
G
, an
r
-incident independent multiset
S
is
genuine
if there exists a set
K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
such that
|K|>1
and
\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K}S(u)
is odd
3
3

3

With a slight abuse of notation,
\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K}S(u)
is the sum over all
u\in V
s.t.
N_{G}(u)=K
.
.

Proposition 55
.

If
G\star^{r}S
is valid and there is no
(r-1)
-incident independent multiset
S^{\prime}
such that
G\star^{r-1}S^{\prime}=G\star^{r}S
, then
S
is a genuine
r
-incident independent multiset.

Proof.

By contradiction, assume
S
is an
r
-incident independent multiset that is not genuine. Let
S^{\prime}
be the multiset obtained from
S
by choosing, for every set
K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
s.t.
\{u\in\textup{{supp}}(S)\penalty\ |\penalty\ N_{G}(u)=K\}
is not empty, a single vertex
u\in\textup{{supp}}(S)
s.t.
N_{G}(u)=K
, and setting
S^{\prime}(u)=\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K}S(u)
and for any other vertex
v\in\textup{{supp}}(S)
s.t.
N_{G}(v)=K
,
S^{\prime}(v)=0
. It is direct to show that
S^{\prime}
is
r
-incident and that
G\star^{r}S=G\star^{r}S^{\prime}
. Then, let
S^{\prime}/2
be the multiset obtained from
S^{\prime}
by dividing by 2 the multiplicity of each vertex in
\textup{{supp}}(S^{\prime})
. It is direct to show that
S^{\prime}
is
(r-1)
-incident and that
G\star^{r}S=G\star^{r-1}S^{\prime}/2
.
∎

Genuine
r
-incidence can only occur for multisets whose support is of size at least exponential in
r
.

Lemma 20
.

Given an integer
r>1
, if
S
is a genuine
r
-incident independent multiset of a graph
G
, then
|\textup{{supp}}(S)|\geqslant 2^{r+2}-r-3
.

Proof.

Let
m>1
be the smallest integer such that there exists a set
K_{0}\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
of size
m
such that
S\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K_{0}}
is odd.
Note that by hypothesis there exists such an integer. Indeed, let
K_{\text{max}}
be the biggest subset (by inclusion) of
V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
such that
\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K_{\text{max}}}S(u)
is odd: then
S\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K_{\text{max}}}
is odd. Thus, by definition of the
r
-incidence,
m\geqslant r+2
.

Let
G^{\prime}=G[\textup{{supp}}(S)\cup K_{0}]
be the graph obtained from
G
by removing the vertices that are neither in the support of
S
, nor in
K_{0}
. By definition,
S
is also
r
-incident in
G^{\prime}
. Also,
S\bullet\Lambda_{G^{\prime}}^{K_{0}}
is odd, and for every set
K\varsubsetneq K_{0}
s.t.
|K|>1
,
S\bullet\Lambda_{G^{\prime}}^{K}
is even.

Let us prove that for any
K\subseteq K_{0}
s.t.
|K|>1
,
\sum_{N_{G^{\prime}}(u)=K}S(u)
is odd, by induction over the size of
K
. First notice that
\sum_{N_{G^{\prime}}(u)=K_{0}}S(u)=S\bullet\Lambda_{G^{\prime}}^{K_{0}}
is odd. Then, let
K_{1}\varsubsetneq K_{0}
s.t.
|K_{1}|>1
.

\displaystyle S\bullet\Lambda_{G^{\prime}}^{K_{1}}=\sum_{K_{1}\subseteq K\subseteq K_{0}}\sum_{N_{G^{\prime}}(u)=K}S(u)=\sum_{N_{G^{\prime}}(u)=K_{1}}S(u)+\sum_{K_{1}\varsubsetneq K\subseteq K_{0}}\sum_{N_{G^{\prime}}(u)=K}S(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{N_{G^{\prime}}(u)=K_{1}}S(u)+|\{K\subseteq K_{0}\penalty\ |\penalty\ K_{1}\varsubsetneq K\}|\bmod 2\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ by hypothesis of induction}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N_{G^{\prime}}(u)=K_{1}}S(u)+1\bmod 2

Thus,
\sum_{N_{G^{\prime}}(u)=K_{1}}S(u)
is odd. As a consequence, for any
K\subseteq K_{0}
s.t.
|K|>1
, there exists at least one vertex
u\in\textup{{supp}}(S)
s.t.
N_{G^{\prime}}(u)=K
. Then,
|\textup{{supp}}(S)|\geqslant|\{K\subseteq K_{0}\penalty\ |\penalty\ |K|>1\}|=2^{m}-m-1\geqslant 2^{r+2}-(r+2)-1=2^{r+2}-r-3
.
∎

Likewise,
r
-local complementations that cannot be implemented by
(r-1)
-local complementations can only occur over multisets with sufficiently many vertices outside their support.

Lemma 21
.

If
G\star^{r}S
is valid and there is no
(r-1)
-incident independent multiset
S^{\prime}
such that
G\star^{r-1}S^{\prime}=G\star^{r}S
, then
|V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)|\geqslant r+3
.

Proof.

According to
Proposition

55
,
S
is genuine. Let
K_{\text{max}}
be one of the biggest subset (by inclusion) of
V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
such that
\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K_{\text{max}}}S(u)
is odd: then
S\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K_{\text{max}}}
is odd. If
|V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)|\leqslant r+1
, then
|K_{max}|\leqslant r+1
, contradicting the
r
-incidence of
S
. Thus,
|V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)|\geqslant|K_{max}|\geqslant r+2
.

Now, suppose
|V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)|=r+2
, i.e.
K_{max}=V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
. Let us prove that for any
K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
s.t.
|K|>1
,
\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K}S(u)
is odd, by induction over the size of
K
. First, notice that
\sum_{N_{G}(u)=V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)}S(u)
is odd.
Then, let
K_{0}\varsubsetneq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
s.t.
|K_{0}|>1
.

\displaystyle S\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K_{0}}=\!\!\!\!\sum_{K_{0}\subseteq K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)}\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K}S(u)=\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K_{0}}S(u)+\sum_{K_{0}\varsubsetneq K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)}\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K}S(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K_{0}}S(u)+|\{K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)\penalty\ |\penalty\ K_{0}\varsubsetneq K\}|\bmod 2\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ by hypothesis of induction}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K_{0}}S(u)+1\bmod 2

Thus,
\sum_{N_{G}(u)=K_{0}}S(u)
is odd, as
S\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K_{0}}
is even by
r
-incidence of
S
. As a consequence, for any
K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
s.t.
|K|>1
, there exists at least one vertex
u\in\textup{{supp}}(S)
s.t.
N_{G}(u)=K
.

Let
S^{\prime}
be the multiset obtained from
S
by choosing, for every
K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
s.t.
|K|>1
, a single vertex
u\in\textup{{supp}}(S)
s.t.
N_{G}(u)=K
, and setting
S^{\prime}(u)=S(u)-1
(the multiplicity of other vertices remain unchanged). Let us prove that
S^{\prime}
is
r
-incident and
G\star^{r}S=G\star^{r}S^{\prime}
. First,
S^{\prime}
is
r
-incident. Indeed, let an integer
k\in[0,r)
, let
K_{1}\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S^{\prime})
be a set of size
k+2
, and let
k^{\prime}=k-|K_{1}\cap\textup{{supp}}(S)|
.
S\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K_{1}}
is a multiple of
2^{r-k^{\prime}-\delta(k^{\prime})}
by
r
-incidence of
S
, so is
S^{\prime}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K_{1}}
, as
S^{\prime}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K_{1}}=S\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K_{1}}-|\{K\subseteq V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)\penalty\ |\penalty\ K_{1}\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)\subseteq K\}|=S\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{K_{1}}-2^{r-k^{\prime}}
. Then, if
u
or
v
is in
\textup{{supp}}(S)
,
u\sim_{G\star^{r}S}v\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ u\sim_{G\star^{r}S^{\prime}}v\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ u\sim_{G}v
. If
u,v\in V\setminus\textup{{supp}}(S)
:

\displaystyle u\sim_{G\star^{r}S}v

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \left(u\sim_{G}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ S\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{u,v}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \left(u\sim_{G}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ S^{\prime}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{u,v}+2^{r}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \left(u\sim_{G}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \oplus\penalty\ \penalty\ S^{\prime}\bullet\Lambda_{G}^{u,v}=2^{r-1}\bmod 2^{r}\right)\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ u\sim_{G\star^{r}S^{\prime}}v

Thus,
G\star^{r}S=G\star^{r}S^{\prime}
. Notice also that
S^{\prime}
is not genuine. Thus, by
Proposition

55
there exists an
S^{\prime\prime}
such that
G\star^{r-1}S^{\prime\prime}=G\star^{r}S^{\prime}=G\star^{r}S
.
∎

Lemmas

20
and
21
together give a simple bound involving only the order of the graph.

Proposition 56
.

If
G\star^{r}S
is valid and there is no
(r-1)
-incident independent multiset
S^{\prime}
such that
G\star^{r-1}S^{\prime}=G\star^{r}S
, then
n\geqslant 2^{r+2}
, where
n
is the order of
G
.

Put differently, any
r
-local complementation over a graph of order at most
2^{r+2}-1
can be implemented by
(r-1)
-local complementations:

Corollary 10
.

If two graphs of order at most
2^{r+2}-1
are LC
r
-equivalent, then they are LC
r-1
-equivalent.

In other words, two LC
r
-equivalent but not LC
r-1
-equivalent graphs are of order at least
2^{r+2}
. This implies the following strengthening of
Theorem

6
.

Corollary 11
.

If two graphs of order at most
2^{r+3}-1
are LU-equivalent, they are LC
r
-equivalent.

Proof.

Suppose that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
of order
n\leqslant 2^{r+3}-1
are LU-equivalent. According to
Theorem

6
,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are LC
n
-equivalent. If
n\leqslant r
then
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are trivially LC
r
-equivalent. Otherwise, according to
Corollary

10
,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are LC
r
-equivalent by direct induction.
∎

Corollary

11
provides a logarithmic bound on the level of generalized local complementations to consider for LU-equivalence, hence the following strengthening of
Theorem

6
.

Theorem 9
.

If two graph states on
n>7
qubits are LU-equivalent, then they are LC
r
-equivalent for some
r\leqslant\lceil\log_{2}(\frac{n+1}{8})\rceil
. (Two LU-equivalent graph states on
n\leqslant 7
qubits are LC-equivalent.)

This bound leads to a quasi-polynomial time algorithm for LU-equivalence, as described in the next section. In
Section

6.4
, we elaborate on the consequences of
Theorem

9
(or equivalently,
Corollary

11
) on the minimal order of graphs that are LU- but not LC-equivalent.

5.4
An algorithm to recognize LU-equivalent graph states

According to
Theorem

8
, we have an algorithm that recognizes two LC
r
-equivalent graphs of order
n
in runtime
O(rn^{r+2.38}+n^{6.38})
. According to
Theorem

9
,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are LU-equivalent if and only if they are LC
r
-equivalent, where
r=\log_{2}(n)+O(1)
. Thus, our algorithm that decides LC
r
-equivalence translates directly to an algorithm that decides LU-equivalence.

Theorem 10
.

There exists an algorithm that decides if two graph states are LU-equivalent with runtime
n^{\log_{2}(n)+O(1)}
, where
n
is number of qubits.

Chapter 6
Sufficient conditions for LU=LC

We say that LU=LC holds for a graph
G
if
G=_{LU}G^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow G=_{LC}G^{\prime}
for any graph
G^{\prime}
. LU=LC implies that:

•

​the LU-orbit of a graph state can be explored with local complementations;

•

​LU-equivalence can be decided efficiently ​(using Bouchet’s algorithm for LC-equivalence).

The 27-vertex graphs that are LU-equivalent but not LC-equivalent are an example of graphs for which LU=LC does not hold. However, many useful classes of graph states satisfy LU=LC. We review in
Section

6.1
and
Section

6.2
known classes of graphs for which LU=LC holds. In
Section

6.3
we use techniques adapted from
Chapter

4
to show that LU=LC holds for yet another useful class of graph states, some instances of repeater graph states, which was conjectured in
[
80
]
. Additionally, in
Section

6.4
, using results from
Chapter

5
, we prove that LU=LC holds for graph states containing up to 19 qubits.

6.1
